Legal moves: f3g5, f3e5, f3h4, f3d4, f3g1, c3d5, c3b5, c3a4, c3e2, c3b1, h1g1, f1a6, f1b5, f1c4, f1d3, f1e2, e1e2, d1e2, a1b1, h2h3, g2g3, d2d3, b2b3, a2a3, h2h4, g2g4, d2d4, b2b4, a2a4.

Yes, c3b5 is legal.

One last check:
White Knight c3 -> b5.
Black Queen a5 is attacked.
Black's only options are to move the Queen or capture.
Capture (Qxb5) leads to Bxb5, winning the Queen.
Moving the Queen (e.g., Qa6) gives White a tempo and a strong knight on b5.

This seems like a professional and strong move.

Wait, let me check if f3e5 was actually a blunder.
f3e5 captures the pawn on e5.
Is the pawn on e5 really defended by the Queen on a5?
Queen: a5 = (0, 4)
Pawn: e5 = (4, 4)
Same rank (y=4).
Yes, the Queen defends e5.
So f3xe5 ... Qxe5. Correct.

What about d2d4?
If 1. d2d4, it challenges the center.
If 1... cxd4, then 2. Nxd4.
If 1... exd4, then 2. Nxd4.
This is also very good.

But c3b5 is more forcing.
I'll choose c3b5.
